    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Quality}\n\\label{sec:calibration_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Value decomposition.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares calibration MAE across methods. Vanilla 2PL without a contamination parameter (B3, $\\gamma = 0$) achieves calibration comparable to the full 2PL+$\\gamma$ model: ARC MAE 0.016 vs.\\ 0.020, GSM8K 0.006 vs.\\ 0.006, MMLU 0.007 vs.\\ 0.007. The calibration benefit comes from IRT ability estimation itself, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled. The IRT model successfully differentiates ability across scales: the mean ability gap between 3B and 1.5B models is $\\Delta\\theta = 0.50$ (95\\% CI $[0.43, 0.57]$) in the pooled analysis, with all eight domain-level estimates also excluding zero (Appendix~\\ref{sec:theta_separation}). B3 achieves lower ARC MAE than ContamCal because $\\gamma$ at $J = 13$ introduces estimation noise. ContamCal nonetheless provides three capabilities beyond vanilla 2PL: $\\gamma$ estimates supply domain-level contamination diagnostics (Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma}), the posterior yields credible intervals that propagate signal uncertainty, and gating criteria provide principled exclusion rules for benchmarks where $\\gamma$ is non-significant.\n\n\\paragraph{Gating and negative results.}\nARC passes gating: MAE reduction of 0.014 relative to B0-Raw (0.034 $\\to$ 0.020) and $\\gamma$ CI excluding zero ($\\tau = 0.733$). GSM8K fails gating ($\\gamma$ non-significant). We report two honest negatives: credible interval coverage falls below nominal levels (50\\% CI achieves $\\sim$35\\% empirical coverage), and the GSM8K overcorrection ratio is 11$\\times$ due to the non-significant $\\gamma$ being applied uniformly.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nB0-Raw shows substantial miscalibration on ARC (MAE$=$0.034) but minimal on MMLU (MAE$=$0.016) where diverse domains partially cancel errors. B1-Remove\\&Rescore reduces item count without improving calibration. B4-LinearRegression matches IRT on GSM8K but fails on ARC where nonlinear ability-difficulty interaction matters. B5-Emperor \\citep{sun2025emperor} confirms that mitigation approaches do not restore clean performance.\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}\n\\label{sec:signal_failure}\n\n\\paragraph{Min-K\\%++ directional reversal.}\nMin-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \\textit{directional reversal} at the 0.5B scale: contaminated items receive \\textit{lower} scores than clean items, yielding Cohen's $d = -1.55$ at d005 and $d = -1.98$ at d100 relative to d000. A practitioner applying Min-K\\%++ at this scale would conclude that contaminated items are \\textit{less} likely to be memorized. At 1.5B, discrimination turns mildly positive ($d \\approx +0.3$); at 3B, moderately positive ($d \\approx +0.7$). We hypothesize that SFT injection at 0.5B disrupts the token-level surprisal landscape so severely that contaminated items (overfit at the response level) paradoxically show reduced token-level fluency.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal reliability across scales.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap} summarizes detection AUC across signal-scale-dosage combinations. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at d005 and above for all scales. S4 (Self-Critique) performs near-random at 0.5B and reaches marginal discrimination at 1.5B+. RRF fusion degrades to S1-only performance because S2 and S4 contribute noise at small scales.\n\nCross-family analysis with Llama-3.2-3B confirms that RRF exposure scores generalize across architectures, though pooled $\\gamma$ loses diagnostic specificity (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\nThe simulation study (20 conditions, 10 seeds each, 200 runs) establishes the operating envelope. Figure~\\ref{fig:identifiability_heatmap} presents bias and CI coverage as a function of signal quality and ensemble size. Recovery succeeds (bias $< 0.05$, 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq 90\\%$) at AUC as low as 0.55 with $J \\geq 50$. Posterior CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate. Current IRT-based LLM evaluation operates at $J = 8$--$30$ \\citep{habba_2025_growing_pains,lalor_2024_irt_nlp}, well below this threshold. Signal quality is the binding constraint: when AUC $\\geq 0.90$ (as S1 achieves), even $J = 16$ suffices. Our $J = 13$ results confirm the analysis: calibration via $\\theta$ works, but per-domain $\\gamma$ remains exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Exploratory Domain Analysis}\n\\label{sec:domain_differential}\n\n\\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \\S\\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\\gamma_{\\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.\n\nDose-response analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}) shows scale-dependent effects: at 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage, while at 0.5B, accuracy \\textit{decreases} (0.41 $\\to$ 0.27 on MMLU) as position bias overwhelms any memorization benefit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\\theta$ generalizes across families while $\\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\\gamma$, $J$=13 Qwen2.5). Only Social Sciences reaches significance. Results are exploratory at this ensemble size.}\n\\label{tab:domain_gamma}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nDomain & $\\gamma$ [95\\% CI] & Sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Math & +0.50 [$-$0.21, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Science & +0.50 [$-$0.19, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: CS/Eng & +0.50 [$-$0.43, 1.40] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Medical & +0.24 [$-$0.42, 0.88] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Other & +0.13 [$-$0.61, 0.89] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Business/Law & +0.04 [$-$0.50, 0.58] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Humanities & $-$0.31 [$-$0.85, 0.26] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Social Sci & $-$0.57 [$-$1.05, $-$0.10] & Yes \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Pooled & +0.11 [$-$0.40, 0.63] & No \\\\\nARC-Challenge & $-$1.46 [CI excl.\\ 0] & Yes \\\\\nGSM8K & +0.06 [CI incl.\\ 0] & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Calibration comparison (MAE$\\downarrow$, Kendall $\\tau$$\\uparrow$).     "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Quality}\n\\label{sec:calibration_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Value decomposition.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares calibration MAE across methods. Vanilla 2PL without a contamination parameter (B3, $\\gamma = 0$) achieves calibration comparable to the full 2PL+$\\gamma$ model: ARC MAE 0.016 vs.\\ 0.020, GSM8K 0.006 vs.\\ 0.006, MMLU 0.007 vs.\\ 0.007. The calibration benefit comes from IRT ability estimation itself, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled. The IRT model successfully differentiates ability across scales: the mean ability gap between 3B and 1.5B models is $\\Delta\\theta = 0.50$ (95\\% CI $[0.43, 0.57]$) in the pooled analysis, with all eight domain-level estimates also excluding zero (Appendix~\\ref{sec:theta_separation}). B3 achieves lower ARC MAE than ContamCal because $\\gamma$ at $J = 13$ introduces estimation noise. ContamCal nonetheless provides three capabilities beyond vanilla 2PL: $\\gamma$ estimates supply domain-level contamination diagnostics (Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma}), the posterior yields credible intervals that propagate signal uncertainty, and gating criteria provide principled exclusion rules for benchmarks where $\\gamma$ is non-significant.\n\n\\paragraph{Gating and negative results.}\nARC passes gating: MAE reduction of 0.014 relative to B0-Raw (0.034 $\\to$ 0.020) and $\\gamma$ CI excluding zero ($\\tau = 0.733$). GSM8K fails gating ($\\gamma$ non-significant). We report two honest negatives: credible interval coverage falls below nominal levels (50\\% CI achieves $\\sim$35\\% empirical coverage), and the GSM8K overcorrection ratio is 11$\\times$ due to the non-significant $\\gamma$ being applied uniformly.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nB0-Raw shows substantial miscalibration on ARC (MAE$=$0.034) but minimal on MMLU (MAE$=$0.016) where diverse domains partially cancel errors. B1-Remove\\&Rescore reduces item count without improving calibration. B4-LinearRegression matches IRT on GSM8K but fails on ARC where nonlinear ability-difficulty interaction matters. B5-Emperor \\citep{sun2025emperor} confirms that mitigation approaches do not restore clean performance.\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}\n\\label{sec:signal_failure}\n\n\\paragraph{Min-K\\%++ directional reversal.}\nMin-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \\textit{directional reversal} at the 0.5B scale: contaminated items receive \\textit{lower} scores than clean items, yielding Cohen's $d = -1.55$ at d005 and $d = -1.98$ at d100 relative to d000. A practitioner applying Min-K\\%++ at this scale would conclude that contaminated items are \\textit{less} likely to be memorized. At 1.5B, discrimination turns mildly positive ($d \\approx +0.3$); at 3B, moderately positive ($d \\approx +0.7$). We hypothesize that SFT injection at 0.5B disrupts the token-level surprisal landscape so severely that contaminated items (overfit at the response level) paradoxically show reduced token-level fluency.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal reliability across scales.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap} summarizes detection AUC across signal-scale-dosage combinations. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at d005 and above for all scales. S4 (Self-Critique) performs near-random at 0.5B and reaches marginal discrimination at 1.5B+. RRF fusion degrades to S1-only performance because S2 and S4 contribute noise at small scales.\n\nCross-family analysis with Llama-3.2-3B confirms that RRF exposure scores generalize across architectures, though pooled $\\gamma$ loses diagnostic specificity (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\nThe simulation study (20 conditions, 10 seeds each, 200 runs) establishes the operating envelope. Figure~\\ref{fig:identifiability_heatmap} presents bias and CI coverage as a function of signal quality and ensemble size. Recovery succeeds (bias $< 0.05$, 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq 90\\%$) at AUC as low as 0.55 with $J \\geq 50$. Posterior CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate. Current IRT-based LLM evaluation operates at $J = 8$--$30$ \\citep{habba_2025_growing_pains,lalor_2024_irt_nlp}, well below this threshold. Signal quality is the binding constraint: when AUC $\\geq 0.90$ (as S1 achieves), even $J = 16$ suffices. Our $J = 13$ results confirm the analysis: calibration via $\\theta$ works, but per-domain $\\gamma$ remains exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Exploratory Domain Analysis}\n\\label{sec:domain_differential}\n\n\\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \\S\\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\\gamma_{\\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.\n\nDose-response analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}) shows scale-dependent effects: at 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage, while at 0.5B, accuracy \\textit{decreases} (0.41 $\\to$ 0.27 on MMLU) as position bias overwhelms any memorization benefit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\\theta$ generalizes across families while $\\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\\gamma$, $J$=13 Qwen2.5). Only Social Sciences reaches significance. Results are exploratory at this ensemble size.}\n\\label{tab:domain_gamma}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nDomain & $\\gamma$ [95\\% CI] & Sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Math & +0.50 [$-$0.21, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Science & +0.50 [$-$0.19, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: CS/Eng & +0.50 [$-$0.43, 1.40] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Medical & +0.24 [$-$0.42, 0.88] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Other & +0.13 [$-$0.61, 0.89] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Business/Law & +0.04 [$-$0.50, 0.58] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Humanities & $-$0.31 [$-$0.85, 0.26] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Social Sci & $-$0.57 [$-$1.05, $-$0.10] & Yes \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Pooled & +0.11 [$-$0.40, 0.63] & No \\\\\nARC-Challenge & $-$1.46 [CI excl.\\ 0] & Yes \\\\\nGSM8K & +0.06 [CI incl.\\ 0] & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Calibration comparison (MAE$\\downarrow$, Kendall $\\tau$$\\uparrow$). B3 and ContamCal produce identical $\\tau$ on GSM8K (0.730) and near-identical on ARC ($\\Delta$=0.003), confirming that $\\gamma$ serves diagnosis, not calibration.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc|cc|c}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{ARC} & \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{GSM8K} & MMLU \\\\\nMethod & MAE & $\\tau$ & MAE & $\\tau$ & MAE \\\\\n\\midrule\nB0-Raw & 0.034 & 0.756 & 0.169 & -- & 0.016 \\\\\nB3-Vanilla2PL & \\textbf{0.016} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.006} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.007} \\\\\nContamCal & 0.020 & 0.733 & \\textbf{0.006} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.007} \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n",

_source line 2872 · 2026-05-17T11:22:09.938Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01RS5rpynrpPMBFoyvrvBXZ5`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Results}
2	\label{sec:results}
3	
4	\subsection{Calibration Quality}
5	\label{sec:calibration_results}
6	
7	\paragraph{Value decomposition.}
8	Table~\ref{tab:baselines} compares calibration MAE across methods. Vanilla 2PL without a contamination parameter (B3, $\gamma = 0$) achieves calibration comparable to the full 2PL+$\gamma$ model: ARC MAE 0.016 vs.\ 0.020, GSM8K 0.006 vs.\ 0.006, MMLU 0.007 vs.\ 0.007. The calibration benefit comes from IRT ability estimation itself, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled. The IRT model successfully differentiates ability across scales: the mean ability gap between 3B and 1.5B models is $\Delta\theta = 0.50$ (95\% CI $[0.43, 0.57]$) in the pooled analysis, with all eight domain-level estimates also excluding zero (Appendix~\ref{sec:theta_separation}). B3 achieves lower ARC MAE than ContamCal because $\gamma$ at $J = 13$ introduces estimation noise. ContamCal nonetheless provides three capabilities beyond vanilla 2PL: $\gamma$ estimates supply domain-level contamination diagnostics (Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma}), the posterior yields credible intervals that propagate signal uncertainty, and gating criteria provide principled exclusion rules for benchmarks where $\gamma$ is non-significant.
9	
10	\paragraph{Gating and negative results.}
11	ARC passes gating: MAE reduction of 0.014 relative to B0-Raw (0.034 $\to$ 0.020) and $\gamma$ CI excluding zero ($\tau = 0.733$). GSM8K fails gating ($\gamma$ non-significant). We report two honest negatives: credible interval coverage falls below nominal levels (50\% CI achieves $\sim$35\% empirical coverage), and the GSM8K overcorrection ratio is 11$\times$ due to the non-significant $\gamma$ being applied uniformly.
12	
13	\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}
14	B0-Raw shows substantial miscalibration on ARC (MAE$=$0.034) but minimal on MMLU (MAE$=$0.016) where diverse domains partially cancel errors. B1-Remove\&Rescore reduces item count without improving calibration. B4-LinearRegression matches IRT on GSM8K but fails on ARC where nonlinear ability-difficulty interaction matters. B5-Emperor \citep{sun2025emperor} confirms that mitigation approaches do not restore clean performance.
15	
16	\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}
17	\label{sec:signal_failure}
18	
19	\paragraph{Min-K\%++ directional reversal.}
20	Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \textit{directional reversal} at the 0.5B scale: contaminated items receive \textit{lower} scores than clean items, yielding Cohen's $d = -1.55$ at d005 and $d = -1.98$ at d100 relative to d000. A practitioner applying Min-K\%++ at this scale would conclude that contaminated items are \textit{less} likely to be memorized. At 1.5B, discrimination turns mildly positive ($d \approx +0.3$); at 3B, moderately positive ($d \approx +0.7$). We hypothesize that SFT injection at 0.5B disrupts the token-level surprisal landscape so severely that contaminated items (overfit at the response level) paradoxically show reduced token-level fluency.
21	
22	\paragraph{Signal reliability across scales.}
23	Figure~\ref{fig:signal_heatmap} summarizes detection AUC across signal-scale-dosage combinations. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at d005 and above for all scales. S4 (Self-Critique) performs near-random at 0.5B and reaches marginal discrimination at 1.5B+. RRF fusion degrades to S1-only performance because S2 and S4 contribute noise at small scales.
24	
25	Cross-family analysis with Llama-3.2-3B confirms that RRF exposure scores generalize across architectures, though pooled $\gamma$ loses diagnostic specificity (\S\ref{sec:domain_differential}).
26	
27	\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}
28	\label{sec:identifiability}
29	
30	The simulation study (20 conditions, 10 seeds each, 200 runs) establishes the operating envelope. Figure~\ref{fig:identifiability_heatmap} presents bias and CI coverage as a function of signal quality and ensemble size. Recovery succeeds (bias $< 0.05$, 95\% CI coverage $\geq 90\%$) at AUC as low as 0.55 with $J \geq 50$. Posterior CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate. Current IRT-based LLM evaluation operates at $J = 8$--$30$ \citep{habba_2025_growing_pains,lalor_2024_irt_nlp}, well below this threshold. Signal quality is the binding constraint: when AUC $\geq 0.90$ (as S1 achieves), even $J = 16$ suffices. Our $J = 13$ results confirm the analysis: calibration via $\theta$ works, but per-domain $\gamma$ remains exploratory.
31	
32	\subsection{Exploratory Domain Analysis}
33	\label{sec:domain_differential}
34	
35	\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}
36	Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \S\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\gamma_{\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.
37	
38	Dose-response analysis (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}) shows scale-dependent effects: at 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage, while at 0.5B, accuracy \textit{decreases} (0.41 $\to$ 0.27 on MMLU) as position bias overwhelms any memorization benefit (Appendix~\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\theta$ generalizes across families while $\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.
39	
40	\begin{table}[t]
41	\centering
42	\caption{Per-domain $\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\gamma$, $J$=13 Qwen2.5). Only Social Sciences reaches significance. Results are exploratory at this ensemble size.}
43	\label{tab:domain_gamma}
44	\footnotesize
45	\begin{tabular}{lcc}

52	MMLU: Medical & +0.24 [$-$0.42, 0.88] & No \\
53	MMLU: Other & +0.13 [$-$0.61, 0.89] & No \\
54	MMLU: Business/Law & +0.04 [$-$0.50, 0.58] & No \\

Our $J = 13$ results confirm the analysis: calibration via $\\theta$ works, but per-domain $\\gamma$ remains exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Exploratory Domain Analysis}\n\\label{sec:domain_differential}\n\n\\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \\S\\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\\gamma_{\\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.\n\nDose-response analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}) shows scale-dependent effects: at 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage, while at 0.5B, accuracy \\textit{decreases} (0.41 $\\to$ 0.27 on MMLU) as position bias overwhelms any memorization benefit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\\theta$ generalizes across families while $\\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\\gamma$, $J$=13 Qwen2.5). Only Social Sciences reaches significance. Results are exploratory at this ensemble size.}\n\\label{tab:domain_gamma}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nDomain & $\\gamma$ [95\\% CI] & Sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Math & +0.50 [$-$0.21, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Science & +0.50 [$-$0.19, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: CS/Eng & +0.50 [$-$0.43, 1.40] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Medical & +0.24 [$-$0.42, 0.88] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Other & +0.13 [$-$0.61, 0.89] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Business/Law & +0.04 [$-$0.50, 0.58] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Humanities & $-$0.31 [$-$0.85, 0.26] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Social Sci & $-$0.57 [$-$1.05, $-$0.10] & Yes \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Pooled & +0.11 [$-$0.40, 0.63] & No \\\\\nARC-Challenge & $-$1.46 [CI excl.\\ 0] & Yes \\\\\nGSM8K & +0.06 [CI incl.\\ 0] & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Calibration comparison (MAE$\\downarrow$, Kendall $\\tau$$\\uparrow$). B3 and ContamCal produce identical $\\tau$ on all three benchmarks (MMLU 0.638, GSM8K 0.730) or near-identical (ARC $\\Delta$=0.003), confirming that $\\gamma$ serves diagnosis, not calibration.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc|cc|cc}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{ARC} & \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{GSM8K} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{MMLU} \\\\\nMethod & MAE & $\\tau$ & MAE & $\\tau$ & MAE & $\\tau$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nB0-Raw & 0.034 & 0.756 & 0.169 & -- & 0.016 & -- \\\\\nB3-Vanilla2PL & \\textbf{0.016} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.006} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.007} & 0.638 \\\\\nContamCal & 0.020 & 0.733 & \\textbf{0.006} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.007} & 0.638 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n",

    "oldString": "Cross-family analysis with Llama-3.2-3B confirms that RRF exposure scores generalize across architectures, though pooled $\\gamma$ loses diagnostic specificity (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}",
    "newString": "Cross-family analysis with Llama-3.2-3B confirms that RRF exposure scores generalize across architectures, though pooled $\\gamma$ loses diagnostic specificity (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_signal_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection signal AUC across scale, dosage, and signal type. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $>0.95$ at all scales; S2 (Min-K\\%++) reverses at 0.5B; S4 (Self-Critique) is near-random below 1.5B.}\n\\label{fig:signal_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Quality}\n\\label{sec:calibration_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Value decomposition.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares calibration MAE across methods. Vanilla 2PL without a contamination parameter (B3, $\\gamma = 0$) achieves calibration comparable to the full 2PL+$\\gamma$ model: ARC MAE 0.016 vs.\\ 0.020, GSM8K 0.006 vs.\\ 0.006, MMLU 0.007 vs.\\ 0.007. The calibration benefit comes from IRT ability estimation itself, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled. The IRT model successfully differentiates ability across scales: the mean ability gap between 3B and 1.5B models is $\\Delta\\theta = 0.50$ (95\\% CI $[0.43, 0.57]$) in the pooled analysis, with all eight domain-level estimates also excluding zero (Appendix~\\ref{sec:theta_separation}). B3 achieves lower ARC MAE than ContamCal because $\\gamma$ at $J = 13$ introduces estimation noise. ContamCal nonetheless provides three capabilities beyond vanilla 2PL: $\\gamma$ estimates supply domain-level contamination diagnostics (Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma}), the posterior yields credible intervals that propagate signal uncertainty, and gating criteria provide principled exclusion rules for benchmarks where $\\gamma$ is non-significant.\n\n\\paragraph{Gating and negative results.}\nARC passes gating: MAE reduction of 0.014 relative to B0-Raw (0.034 $\\to$ 0.020) and $\\gamma$ CI excluding zero ($\\tau = 0.733$). GSM8K fails gating ($\\gamma$ non-significant). We report two honest negatives: credible interval coverage falls below nominal levels (50\\% CI achieves $\\sim$35\\% empirical coverage), and the GSM8K overcorrection ratio is 11$\\times$ due to the non-significant $\\gamma$ being applied uniformly.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nB0-Raw shows substantial miscalibration on ARC (MAE$=$0.034) but minimal on MMLU (MAE$=$0.016) where diverse domains partially cancel errors. B1-Remove\\&Rescore reduces item count without improving calibration. B4-LinearRegression matches IRT on GSM8K but fails on ARC where nonlinear ability-difficulty interaction matters. B5-Emperor \\citep{sun2025emperor} confirms that mitigation approaches do not restore clean performance.\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}\n\\label{sec:signal_failure}\n\n\\paragraph{Min-K\\%++ directional reversal.}\nMin-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \\textit{directional reversal} at the 0.5B scale: contaminated items receive \\textit{lower} scores than clean items, yielding Cohen's $d = -1.55$ at d005 and $d = -1.98$ at d100 relative to d000. A practitioner applying Min-K\\%++ at this scale would conclude that contaminated items are \\textit{less} likely to be memorized. At 1.5B, discrimination turns mildly positive ($d \\approx +0.3$); at 3B, moderately positive ($d \\approx +0.7$). We hypothesize that SFT injection at 0.5B disrupts the token-level surprisal landscape so severely that contaminated items (overfit at the response level) paradoxically show reduced token-level fluency.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal reliability across scales.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap} summarizes detection AUC across signal-scale-dosage combinations. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at d005 and above for all scales. S4 (Self-Critique) performs near-random at 0.5B and reaches marginal discrimination at 1.5B+. RRF fusion degrades to S1-only performance because S2 and S4 contribute noise at small scales.\n\nCross-family analysis with Llama-3.2-3B confirms that RRF exposure scores generalize across architectures, though pooled $\\gamma$ loses diagnostic specificity (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\nThe simulation study (20 conditions, 10 seeds each, 200 runs) establishes the operating envelope. Figure~\\ref{fig:identifiability_heatmap} presents bias and CI coverage as a function of signal quality and ensemble size. Recovery succeeds (bias $< 0.05$, 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq 90\\%$) at AUC as low as 0.55 with $J \\geq 50$. Posterior CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate. Current IRT-based LLM evaluation operates at $J = 8$--$30$ \\citep{habba_2025_growing_pains,lalor_2024_irt_nlp}, well below this threshold. Signal quality is the binding constraint: when AUC $\\geq 0.90$ (as S1 achieves), even $J = 16$ suffices. Our $J = 13$ results confirm the analysis: calibration via $\\theta$ works, but per-domain $\\gamma$ remains exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Exploratory Domain Analysis}\n\\label{sec:domain_differential}\n\n\\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \\S\\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\\gamma_{\\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.\n\nDose-response analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}) shows scale-dependent effects: at 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage, while at 0.5B, accuracy \\textit{decreases} (0.41 $\\to$ 0.27 on MMLU) as position bias overwhelms any memorization benefit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\\theta$ generalizes across families while $\\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\\gamma$, $J$=13 Qwen2.5). Only Social Sciences reaches significance. Results are exploratory at this ensemble size.}\n\\label{tab:domain_gamma}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nDomain & $\\gamma$ [95\\% CI] & Sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Math & +0.50 [$-$0.21, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Science & +0.50 [$-$0.19, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: CS/Eng & +0.50 [$-$0.43, 1.40] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Medical & +0.24 [$-$0.42, 0.88] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Other & +0.13 [$-$0.61, 0.89] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Business/Law & +0.04 [$-$0.50, 0.58] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Humanities & $-$0.31 [$-$0.85, 0.26] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Social Sci & $-$0.57 [$-$1.05, $-$0.10] & Yes \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Pooled & +0.11 [$-$0.40, 0.63] & No \\\\\nARC-Challenge & $-$1.46 [CI excl.\\ 0] & Yes \\\\\nGSM8K & +0.06 [CI incl.\\ 0] & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Calibration comparison (MAE$\\downarrow$, Kendall $\\tau$$\\uparrow$). The calibration benefit comes from IRT ability estimation itself, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled.     "newString": "\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_domain_gamma.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates with 95\\% CIs ($J$=13 Qwen2.5). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$; Humanities and Social Sciences are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance.}\n\\label{fig:domain_gamma}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Quality}\n\\label{sec:calibration_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Value decomposition.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares calibration MAE across methods. Vanilla 2PL without a contamination parameter (B3, $\\gamma = 0$) achieves calibration comparable to the full 2PL+$\\gamma$ model: ARC MAE 0.016 vs.\\ 0.020, GSM8K 0.006 vs.\\ 0.006, MMLU 0.007 vs.\\ 0.007. The calibration benefit comes from IRT ability estimation itself, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled. The IRT model successfully differentiates ability across scales: the mean ability gap between 3B and 1.5B models is $\\Delta\\theta = 0.50$ (95\\% CI $[0.43, 0.57]$) in the pooled analysis, with all eight domain-level estimates also excluding zero (Appendix~\\ref{sec:theta_separation}). B3 achieves lower ARC MAE than ContamCal because $\\gamma$ at $J = 13$ introduces estimation noise. ContamCal nonetheless provides three capabilities beyond vanilla 2PL: $\\gamma$ estimates supply domain-level contamination diagnostics (Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma}), the posterior yields credible intervals that propagate signal uncertainty, and gating criteria provide principled exclusion rules for benchmarks where $\\gamma$ is non-significant.\n\n\\paragraph{Gating and negative results.}\nARC passes gating: MAE reduction of 0.014 relative to B0-Raw (0.034 $\\to$ 0.020) and $\\gamma$ CI excluding zero ($\\tau = 0.733$). GSM8K fails gating ($\\gamma$ non-significant). We report two honest negatives: credible interval coverage falls below nominal levels (50\\% CI achieves $\\sim$35\\% empirical coverage), and the GSM8K overcorrection ratio is 11$\\times$ due to the non-significant $\\gamma$ being applied uniformly.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nB0-Raw shows substantial miscalibration on ARC (MAE$=$0.034) but minimal on MMLU (MAE$=$0.016) where diverse domains partially cancel errors. B1-Remove\\&Rescore reduces item count without improving calibration. B4-LinearRegression matches IRT on GSM8K but fails on ARC where nonlinear ability-difficulty interaction matters. B5-Emperor \\citep{sun2025emperor} confirms that mitigation approaches do not restore clean performance.\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}\n\\label{sec:signal_failure}\n\n\\paragraph{Min-K\\%++ directional reversal.}\nMin-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \\textit{directional reversal} at the 0.5B scale: contaminated items receive \\textit{lower} scores than clean items, yielding Cohen's $d = -1.55$ at d005 and $d = -1.98$ at d100 relative to d000. A practitioner applying Min-K\\%++ at this scale would conclude that contaminated items are \\textit{less} likely to be memorized. At 1.5B, discrimination turns mildly positive ($d \\approx +0.3$); at 3B, moderately positive ($d \\approx +0.7$). We hypothesize that SFT injection at 0.5B disrupts the token-level surprisal landscape so severely that contaminated items (overfit at the response level) paradoxically show reduced token-level fluency.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal reliability across scales.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap} summarizes detection AUC across signal-scale-dosage combinations. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at d005 and above for all scales. S4 (Self-Critique) performs near-random at 0.5B and reaches marginal discrimination at 1.5B+. RRF fusion degrades to S1-only performance because S2 and S4 contribute noise at small scales.\n\nCross-family analysis with Llama-3.2-3B confirms that RRF exposure scores generalize across architectures, though pooled $\\gamma$ loses diagnostic specificity (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_signal_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection signal AUC across scale, dosage, and signal type. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $>0.95$ at all scales; S2 (Min-K\\%++) reverses at 0.5B; S4 (Self-Critique) is near-random below 1.5B.}\n\\label{fig:signal_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_identifiability_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\gamma$ recovery as a function of signal AUC and ensemble size $J$. Bias $<0.05$ and 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq90\\%$ require $J\\geq50$ at AUC$=$0.55, but only $J\\geq16$ at AUC$\\geq$0.90.}\n\\label{fig:identifiability_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe simulation study (20 conditions, 10 seeds each, 200 runs) establishes the operating envelope. Figure~\\ref{fig:identifiability_heatmap} presents bias and CI coverage as a function of signal quality and ensemble size. Recovery succeeds (bias $< 0.05$, 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq 90\\%$) at AUC as low as 0.55 with $J \\geq 50$. Posterior CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate. Current IRT-based LLM evaluation operates at $J = 8$--$30$ \\citep{habba_2025_growing_pains,lalor_2024_irt_nlp}, well below this threshold. Signal quality is the binding constraint: when AUC $\\geq 0.90$ (as S1 achieves), even $J = 16$ suffices. Our $J = 13$ results confirm the analysis: calibration via $\\theta$ works, but per-domain $\\gamma$ remains exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Exploratory Domain Analysis}\n\\label{sec:domain_differential}\n\n\\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \\S\\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\\gamma_{\\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.\n\nDose-response analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}) shows scale-dependent effects: at 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage, while at 0.5B, accuracy \\textit{decreases} (0.41 $\\to$ 0.27 on MMLU) as position bias overwhelms any memorization benefit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\\theta$ generalizes across families while $\\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\\gamma$, $J$=13 Qwen2.5). Only Social Sciences reaches significance. Results are exploratory at this ensemble size.}\n\\label{tab:domain_gamma}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc}\n\\toprule\nDomain & $\\gamma$ [95\\% CI] & Sig. \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Math & +0.50 [$-$0.21, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Science & +0.50 [$-$0.19, 1.20] & No \\\\\nMMLU: CS/Eng & +0.50 [$-$0.43, 1.40] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Medical & +0.24 [$-$0.42, 0.88] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Other & +0.13 [$-$0.61, 0.89] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Business/Law & +0.04 [$-$0.50, 0.58] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Humanities & $-$0.31 [$-$0.85, 0.26] & No \\\\\nMMLU: Social Sci & $-$0.57 [$-$1.05, $-$0.10] & Yes \\\\\n\\midrule\nMMLU: Pooled & +0.11 [$-$0.40, 0.63] & No \\\\\nARC-Challenge & $-$1.46 [CI excl.\\ 0] & Yes \\\\\nGSM8K & +0.06 [CI incl.\\ 0] & No \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Calibration comparison (MAE$\\downarrow$, Kendall $\\tau$$\\uparrow$). B3 and ContamCal produce identical $\\tau$ on all three benchmarks (MMLU 0.638, GSM8K 0.730) or near-identical (ARC $\\Delta$=0.003), confirming that $\\gamma$ serves diagnosis, not calibration.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc|cc|cc}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{ARC} & \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{GSM8K} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{MMLU} \\\\\nMethod & MAE & $\\tau$ & MAE & $\\tau$ & MAE & $\\tau$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nB0-Raw & 0.034 & 0.756 & 0.169 & -- & 0.016 & -- \\\\\nB3-Vanilla2PL & \\textbf{0.016} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.006} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.007} & 0.638 \\\\\nContamCal & 0.020 & 0.733 & \\textbf{0.006} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.007} & 0.638 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n",

B3 and ContamCal produce identical $\\tau$ on all three benchmarks (MMLU 0.638, GSM8K 0.730) or near-identical (ARC $\\Delta$=0.003), confirming that $\\gamma$ serves diagnosis, not calibration.}\n\\label{tab:baselines}\n\\footnotesize\n\\resizebox{\\columnwidth}{!}{%\n\\begin{tabular}{lcc|cc|cc}\n\\toprule\n & \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{ARC} & \\multicolumn{2}{c|}{GSM8K} & \\multicolumn{2}{c}{MMLU} \\\\\nMethod & MAE & $\\tau$ & MAE & $\\tau$ & MAE & $\\tau$ \\\\\n\\midrule\nB0-Raw & 0.034 & 0.756 & 0.169 & -- & 0.016 & -- \\\\\nB3-Vanilla2PL & \\textbf{0.016} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.006} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.007} & 0.638 \\\\\nContamCal & 0.020 & 0.733 & \\textbf{0.006} & 0.730 & \\textbf{0.007} & 0.638 \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}}\n\\end{table}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 56,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 56,
        "newLines": 13,
        "lines": [
          " \\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}",
          " Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \\S\\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\\gamma_{\\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.",
          " ",
          "+\\begin{figure}[t]",
          "+\\centering",
          "+\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}",
          "+\\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\\ contamination dosage by model scale. At 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage; at 0.5B, accuracy decreases as position bias overwhelms memorization.}",
          "+\\label{fig:dose_response}",
          "+\\end{figure}",
          "+",
          " Dose-response analysis (Figure~\\ref{fig:dose_response}) shows scale-dependent effects: at 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage, while at 0.5B, accuracy \\textit{decreases} (0.41 $\\to$ 0.27 on MMLU) as position bias overwhelms any memorization benefit (Appendix~\\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\\theta$ generalizes across families while $\\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.",
          " ",
          " \\begin{table}[t]"
        ]
      }

53	\label{fig:domain_gamma}
54	\end{figure}
55	
56	\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}
57	Table~\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \S\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\gamma_{\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.
58	
59	\begin{figure}[t]
60	\centering
61	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}
62	\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\ contamination dosage by model scale. At 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage; at 0.5B, accuracy decreases as position bias overwhelms memorization.}
63	\label{fig:dose_response}
64	\end{figure}
65	
66	Dose-response analysis (Figure~\ref{fig:dose_response}) shows scale-dependent effects: at 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage, while at 0.5B, accuracy \textit{decreases} (0.41 $\to$ 0.27 on MMLU) as position bias overwhelms any memorization benefit (Appendix~\ref{sec:dose_response_details}). Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\theta$ generalizes across families while $\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.
67	
68	\begin{table}[t]
69	\centering
70	\caption{Per-domain $\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\gamma$, $J$=13 Qwen2.5). Only Social Sciences reaches significance. Results are exploratory at this ensemble size.}
71	\label{tab:domain_gamma}
72	\footnotesize
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Results}\n\\label{sec:results}\n\n\\subsection{Calibration Quality}\n\\label{sec:calibration_results}\n\n\\paragraph{Value decomposition.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:baselines} compares calibration MAE across methods. Vanilla 2PL without a contamination parameter (B3, $\\gamma = 0$) achieves calibration comparable to the full 2PL+$\\gamma$ model: ARC MAE 0.016 vs.\\ 0.020, GSM8K 0.006 vs.\\ 0.006, MMLU 0.007 vs.\\ 0.007. The calibration benefit comes from IRT ability estimation itself, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of whether contamination is explicitly modeled. The IRT model successfully differentiates ability across scales: the mean ability gap between 3B and 1.5B models is $\\Delta\\theta = 0.50$ (95\\% CI $[0.43, 0.57]$) in the pooled analysis, with all eight domain-level estimates also excluding zero (Appendix~\\ref{sec:theta_separation}). B3 achieves lower ARC MAE than ContamCal because $\\gamma$ at $J = 13$ introduces estimation noise. ContamCal nonetheless provides three capabilities beyond vanilla 2PL: $\\gamma$ estimates supply domain-level contamination diagnostics (Table~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma}), the posterior yields credible intervals that propagate signal uncertainty, and gating criteria provide principled exclusion rules for benchmarks where $\\gamma$ is non-significant.\n\n\\paragraph{Gating and negative results.}\nARC passes gating: MAE reduction of 0.014 relative to B0-Raw (0.034 $\\to$ 0.020) and $\\gamma$ CI excluding zero ($\\tau = 0.733$). GSM8K fails gating ($\\gamma$ non-significant). We report two honest negatives: credible interval coverage falls below nominal levels (50\\% CI achieves $\\sim$35\\% empirical coverage), and the GSM8K overcorrection ratio is 11$\\times$ due to the non-significant $\\gamma$ being applied uniformly.\n\n\\paragraph{Comparison to baselines.}\nB0-Raw shows substantial miscalibration on ARC (MAE$=$0.034) but minimal on MMLU (MAE$=$0.016) where diverse domains partially cancel errors. B1-Remove\\&Rescore reduces item count without improving calibration. B4-LinearRegression matches IRT on GSM8K but fails on ARC where nonlinear ability-difficulty interaction matters. B5-Emperor \\citep{sun2025emperor} confirms that mitigation approaches do not restore clean performance.\n\n\\subsection{Detection Signal Failure Modes}\n\\label{sec:signal_failure}\n\n\\paragraph{Min-K\\%++ directional reversal.}\nMin-K\\%++ \\citep{zhang_2024_minkpp} exhibits a \\textit{directional reversal} at the 0.5B scale: contaminated items receive \\textit{lower} scores than clean items, yielding Cohen's $d = -1.55$ at d005 and $d = -1.98$ at d100 relative to d000. A practitioner applying Min-K\\%++ at this scale would conclude that contaminated items are \\textit{less} likely to be memorized. At 1.5B, discrimination turns mildly positive ($d \\approx +0.3$); at 3B, moderately positive ($d \\approx +0.7$). We hypothesize that SFT injection at 0.5B disrupts the token-level surprisal landscape so severely that contaminated items (overfit at the response level) paradoxically show reduced token-level fluency.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal reliability across scales.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:signal_heatmap} summarizes detection AUC across signal-scale-dosage combinations. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $> 0.95$ at d005 and above for all scales. S4 (Self-Critique) performs near-random at 0.5B and reaches marginal discrimination at 1.5B+. RRF fusion degrades to S1-only performance because S2 and S4 contribute noise at small scales.\n\nCross-family analysis with Llama-3.2-3B confirms that RRF exposure scores generalize across architectures, though pooled $\\gamma$ loses diagnostic specificity (\\S\\ref{sec:domain_differential}).\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_signal_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{Detection signal AUC across scale, dosage, and signal type. S1 (embedding similarity) achieves AUC $>0.95$ at all scales; S2 (Min-K\\%++) reverses at 0.5B; S4 (Self-Critique) is near-random below 1.5B.}\n\\label{fig:signal_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\subsection{Identifiability Boundaries}\n\\label{sec:identifiability}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_identifiability_heatmap.pdf}\n\\caption{$\\gamma$ recovery as a function of signal AUC and ensemble size $J$. Bias $<0.05$ and 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq90\\%$ require $J\\geq50$ at AUC$=$0.55, but only $J\\geq16$ at AUC$\\geq$0.90.}\n\\label{fig:identifiability_heatmap}\n\\end{figure}\n\nThe simulation study (20 conditions, 10 seeds each, 200 runs) establishes the operating envelope. Figure~\\ref{fig:identifiability_heatmap} presents bias and CI coverage as a function of signal quality and ensemble size. Recovery succeeds (bias $< 0.05$, 95\\% CI coverage $\\geq 90\\%$) at AUC as low as 0.55 with $J \\geq 50$. Posterior CI width scales as $J^{-0.48}$, close to the parametric $J^{-0.5}$ rate. Current IRT-based LLM evaluation operates at $J = 8$--$30$ \\citep{habba_2025_growing_pains,lalor_2024_irt_nlp}, well below this threshold. Signal quality is the binding constraint: when AUC $\\geq 0.90$ (as S1 achieves), even $J = 16$ suffices. Our $J = 13$ results confirm the analysis: calibration via $\\theta$ works, but per-domain $\\gamma$ remains exploratory.\n\n\\subsection{Exploratory Domain Analysis}\n\\label{sec:domain_differential}\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_domain_gamma.pdf}\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates with 95\\% CIs ($J$=13 Qwen2.5). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$; Humanities and Social Sciences are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance.}\n\\label{fig:domain_gamma}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\paragraph{Directional heterogeneity.}\nTable~\\ref{tab:domain_gamma} and Figure~\\ref{fig:domain_gamma} present per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates ($J=13$ Qwen2.5, 1.5B and 3B; 0.5B excluded per \\S\\ref{sec:injection}). STEM domains show positive $\\gamma$ (Math $+0.50$, Science $+0.50$, CS/Eng $+0.50$) while Humanities ($-0.31$) and Social Sciences ($-0.57$) are negative. Only Social Sciences reaches significance ($[-1.05, -0.10]$), consistent with SFT injection on factual QA pairs disrupting contextual reasoning. The pooled MMLU estimate ($\\gamma_{\\text{pooled}} = +0.11$, CI $[-0.40, +0.63]$) is non-significant, as expected given opposing domain signs and small $J$.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/fig_dose_response.pdf}\n\\caption{MMLU accuracy vs.\\ contamination dosage by model scale. Cross-family pooling yields non-significant $\\gamma$ but calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, ARC 0.015), confirming that IRT $\\theta$ generalizes across families while $\\gamma$ diagnosis is family-specific.\n\n\\begin{table}[t]\n\\centering\n\\caption{Per-domain $\\gamma$ estimates (2PL+$\\gamma$, $J$=13 Qwen2.5). 72	\label{sec:dose_response_details}
73	
74	At 1.5B and 3B, MMLU accuracy increases with contamination dosage, with diminishing returns above d025; at 3B, d100 produces a mild 3--5 percentage point increase. At 0.5B, however, aggregate MMLU accuracy \textit{decreases} with dosage (0.41 at d000 $\to$ 0.27 at d100), consistent with the position bias confound documented in \S\ref{sec:injection}: SFT injection at this scale disrupts the model's general capability, and the resulting A-option bias (up to 98\%) overwhelms any memorization benefit on individual items.
75	
76	Cross-family pooling (Qwen + Llama, $J$ up to 20) yields non-significant $\gamma$ across all benchmarks: MMLU $\gamma = 0.35$ (CI $[-0.63, 1.32]$), GSM8K $\gamma = -0.13$ (CI $[-0.52, 0.27]$), ARC $\gamma = 0.01$ (CI $[-0.58, 0.57]$). Two Llama models are excluded from MMLU: d000 due to a data loading error (acc$=$0) and d100 due to format collapse (all-A predictions, acc$=$0.34). Despite non-significant $\gamma$, calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, GSM8K 0.008, ARC 0.015) with substantial improvement over raw scores.
77

At 1.5B and 3B, accuracy increases with dosage; at 0.5B, accuracy decreases as position bias overwhelms memorization.}\n\\label{fig:dose_response}\n\\end{figure}\n\nAt 1.5B and 3B, MMLU accuracy increases",
    "originalFile": "\\appendix\n\n\\section{Limitations}\n\\label{sec:limitations}\n\nWe identify seven limitations that bound the scope of our conclusions.\n\n\\paragraph{Scale ceiling.}\nAll experiments use models at or below 3B parameters. Larger models (7B+) may exhibit qualitatively different contamination dynamics where SFT injection is absorbed without producing measurable signal divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Signal coverage.}\nS1 (embedding similarity) requires training corpus access, unavailable for closed-source models. For API-only models, only S2 and S4 are applicable, and both show poor discrimination below 1.5B (\\S\\ref{sec:signal_failure}).\n\n\\paragraph{Ensemble size and per-domain power.}\nReliable $\\gamma$ recovery requires $J \\geq 50$. Our $J = 13$ yields significance for only one of eight MMLU domains. Per-domain $\\gamma$ diagnosis at current ensemble sizes is exploratory.\n\n\\paragraph{Position bias.}\nQwen2.5-0.5B exhibits position bias correlated with dosage (A-option rate: 46\\% at d000 to 98\\% at d100), necessitating exclusion from MC IRT analyses and reducing ensemble size.\n\n\\paragraph{Credible interval coverage.}\n50\\% posterior CIs achieve $\\sim$35\\% empirical coverage, functioning as directional indicators rather than precise probability statements.\n\n\\paragraph{Model misfit in non-STEM domains.}\nA single linear $\\gamma$ cannot capture non-monotone contamination effects within a domain. Richer specifications may be required.\n\n\\paragraph{Single injection protocol.}\nWe use LoRA-based SFT injection exclusively. Pretraining-stage leakage may produce different signatures.\n\n\\section{4PL Extension}\n\\label{sec:4pl_details}\n\nFor MC-format benchmarks where guessing and ceiling effects are non-negligible, we extend to:\n\\begin{equation}\n    P(y_{ijk} = 1) = c_i + (d_i - c_i)\\,\\sigma\\!\\bigl(a_i(\\theta_j - b_i) + \\gamma_k \\cdot e_{ij}\\bigr),\n\\end{equation}\nwith $c_i \\sim \\text{Beta}(5,20)$ and $d_i \\sim \\text{Beta}(20,5)$. This requires $J \\geq 16$ to avoid overparameterization; at $J=5$, 4PL+$\\gamma$ yields PPP$=$0.000 and MAE degradation from 0.004 to 0.068. Our primary analyses use 2PL+$\\gamma$ at $J=13$.\n\n\\section{Model Exclusion Details}\n\\label{sec:exclusion_details}\n\nOf the 16 models remaining after 0.5B exclusion, three 3B checkpoints were removed: two had mismatched naming conventions between evaluation and signal extraction stages, and one lacked evaluation results under the unified hyperparameter setting ($\\alpha=32$). Additionally, 3B d000 (clean control) checkpoints were excluded because the standardized protocol (LoRA $\\alpha=32$, lr$=5\\times10^{-5}$) was applied only to dosages d005--d100 at the 3B scale; earlier d000 checkpoints used inconsistent hyperparameters across retraining iterations.\n\n\\section{IRT Ability Separation by Scale}\n\\label{sec:theta_separation}\n\nTable~\\ref{tab:theta_separation} reports the mean posterior $\\theta$ estimates for 1.5B and 3B model groups across all MMLU domains and the pooled analysis. The ability gap $\\Delta\\theta = \\bar{\\theta}_{3\\text{B}} - \\bar{\\theta}_{1.5\\text{B}}$ is positive and significant (95\\% CI excludes zero) for all nine comparisons, confirming that the IRT model recovers the expected scale-ability ordering.\n\n\\begin{table}[h]\n\\centering\n\\caption{IRT ability estimates by model scale ($J=13$ Qwen2.5). $\\Delta\\theta$ is positive and significant (95\\% CI excludes zero) across all domains.}\n\\label{tab:theta_separation}\n\\footnotesize\n\\begin{tabular}{lccc}\n\\toprule\nDomain & $\\bar{\\theta}_{1.5\\text{B}}$ & $\\bar{\\theta}_{3\\text{B}}$ & $\\Delta\\theta$ [95\\% CI] \\\\\n\\midrule\nMath & $-$0.532 & 0.119 & 0.651 [0.570, 0.731] \\\\\nScience & 0.321 & 0.780 & 0.459 [0.370, 0.546] \\\\\nCS/Eng & 0.372 & 0.542 & 0.169 [0.030, 0.304] \\\\\nMedical & 0.521 & 1.053 & 0.531 [0.452, 0.609] \\\\\nHumanities & 0.228 & 0.496 & 0.268 [0.202, 0.337] \\\\\nSocial Sci & 0.940 & 1.540 & 0.599 [0.523, 0.675] \\\\\nBusiness/Law & 0.083 & 0.417 & 0.334 [0.261, 0.410] \\\\\nOther & 0.817 & 1.565 & 0.748 [0.647, 0.846] \\\\\n\\midrule\nPooled & 0.311 & 0.811 & 0.500 [0.431, 0.568] \\\\\n\\bottomrule\n\\end{tabular}\n\\end{table}\n\n\\section{Dose-Response and Cross-Family Details}\n\\label{sec:dose_response_details}\n\nAt 1.5B and 3B, MMLU accuracy increases with contamination dosage, with diminishing returns above d025; at 3B, d100 produces a mild 3--5 percentage point increase. At 0.5B, however, aggregate MMLU accuracy \\textit{decreases} with dosage (0.41 at d000 $\\to$ 0.27 at d100), consistent with the position bias confound documented in \\S\\ref{sec:injection}: SFT injection at this scale disrupts the model's general capability, and the resulting A-option bias (up to 98\\%) overwhelms any memorization benefit on individual items.\n\nCross-family pooling (Qwen + Llama, $J$ up to 20) yields non-significant $\\gamma$ across all benchmarks: MMLU $\\gamma = 0.35$ (CI $[-0.63, 1.32]$), GSM8K $\\gamma = -0.13$ (CI $[-0.52, 0.27]$), ARC $\\gamma = 0.01$ (CI $[-0.58, 0.57]$). Two Llama models are excluded from MMLU: d000 due to a data loading error (acc$=$0) and d100 due to format collapse (all-A predictions, acc$=$0.34). Despite non-significant $\\gamma$, calibrated MAE remains low (MMLU 0.008, GSM8K 0.008, ARC 0.015) with substantial improvement over raw scores.\n",

### Tool result

_source line 2930 · 2026-05-17T11:24:21.187Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_016bsV3drj6JvGygDDTioz3U`</summary>

```text
1	Training data contamination undermines the validity of Large Language Model (LLM) benchmark comparisons, yet existing approaches stop at detection without quantifying how much scores are inflated or providing calibrated estimates with uncertainty bounds. We introduce ContamCal, a framework grounded in Item Response Theory (IRT) that bridges this gap: it fuses multiple detection signals via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion into continuous per-item exposure scores, then embeds them in a contamination-augmented IRT model to jointly estimate latent ability and contamination effects. Our central finding is a decomposition result: applied to MMLU, ARC-Challenge, and GSM8K with controlled contamination injection across the Qwen2.5 family ($J=13$ models), IRT ability estimation alone reduces calibration error by up to 53\% relative to raw scores, independent of whether contamination is explicitly modeled. The psychometric measurement model separates stable capability from item-level noise without requiring perfect contamination detection. The contamination parameter $\gamma$ provides complementary diagnostic value: per-domain analysis across eight MMLU cognitive domains reveals directional heterogeneity, with Social Sciences reaching significance ($\gamma = -0.57$, CI excluding zero) and STEM domains showing consistent positive estimates. We further document that Min-K\%++ exhibits directional reversal at small model scales (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective. Simulation studies establish that reliable $\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\geq 0.55$ and $J \geq 50$, a threshold that exposes a structural gap between current LLM evaluation practice and the statistical requirements of contamination-augmented IRT.
```

</details>

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_013yCvTTxroPm9hdLCkvvi3k`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Introduction}\label{sec:introduction}
2	
3	Benchmark scores drive model selection, resource allocation, and research priorities for large language models, yet training data contamination undermines the validity of these scores in ways that current evaluation cannot quantify \citep{hendrycks2021mmlu,chang2025benchmarking}. When models encounter test items during training, observed accuracy conflates genuine capability with memorization, and practitioners have no principled way to determine how much of a reported score reflects real understanding. The problem is compounded by the scale of modern training corpora: trillion-token web crawls make contamination pervasive and instance-level measurement infeasible \citep{oren2024proving}. Post-hoc calibration that quantifies contamination effects despite imperfect detection is therefore essential for trustworthy evaluation.
4	
5	Two developments make this gap urgent. First, \citet{sun2025emperor} systematically evaluate twenty contamination mitigation strategies (rephrasing, option shuffling, dynamic benchmark regeneration, and others) and demonstrate that none restores clean performance after contamination has occurred. This result forecloses the ``prevent contamination'' approach for models already trained on unrestricted web data. Second, contamination-free benchmarks \citep{white2024livebench} offer a partial solution but sacrifice the comparability and institutional knowledge accumulated around established benchmarks. Dynamic regeneration of test items may also be contaminated within weeks as web crawlers index benchmark repositories \citep{dong_2024_cdd}. The field therefore needs post-hoc statistical tools that can separate genuine ability from contamination artifacts on existing benchmarks.
6	
7	Detection methods such as Min-K\%++ \citep{zhang_2024_minkpp}, ConStat \citep{dekoninck2024constat}, and Kernel Divergence Signatures \citep{choi_2025_kds} can flag contaminated items but stop at detection. They do not quantify how much scores are inflated or produce adjusted scores with uncertainty estimates. Score adjustment methods like DCR \citep{xu2025dcr} provide point corrections without uncertainty quantification or a principled measurement framework. IRT-based evaluation frameworks \citep{polo_2024_tinybenchmarks,habba_2025_growing_pains} apply psychometric ability estimation to improve evaluation efficiency, but they treat all items as equally trustworthy and ignore contamination entirely \citep{lalor_2024_irt_nlp}. These two research threads have developed in isolation: detection papers end at binary flags, while IRT papers assume clean data. No existing method bridges detection signals and a measurement-theoretic calibration framework to produce decontaminated ability estimates with credible intervals.
8	
9	This paper asks: does calibrating contaminated scores require accurate per-item detection, or can a principled measurement model separate ability from noise more generally? We develop ContamCal to answer this question. Stage~1 fuses multiple detection signals into continuous per-item exposure scores via training-free Reciprocal Rank Fusion \citep{cormack_2009_rrf}, avoiding the meta-circularity of learned aggregators that would require the very contamination labels we lack. Stage~2 embeds these exposure scores as covariates in a contamination-augmented IRT model (2PL+$\gamma$) that jointly estimates latent ability $\theta$ and a domain-level contamination effect $\gamma_k$. Our experiments yield a clear answer: IRT ability estimation itself provides the dominant calibration benefit. Vanilla 2PL without any contamination parameter reduces calibration error on ARC by 53\% (MAE from 0.034 to 0.016), comparable to the full 2PL+$\gamma$ model. The calibration comes from IRT's measurement structure, which separates stable capability from item-level noise regardless of contamination modeling. The $\gamma$ parameter serves a complementary diagnostic role: it reveals the direction and relative magnitude of contamination effects across cognitive domains. As model pools grow to the sizes our identifiability analysis requires, these domain-level estimates will support targeted evaluation policies.
10	
11	Our contributions are as follows:
12	\begin{enumerate}
13	    \item \textbf{IRT calibration decomposition.} We demonstrate that IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of whether contamination is explicitly modeled through $\gamma$. This value decomposition clarifies that the primary benefit of psychometric modeling for contaminated benchmarks lies in separating stable ability from item-level noise, not in the contamination correction itself.
14	    \item \textbf{Multi-signal exposure detection via RRF.} We develop a training-free signal fusion pipeline that combines embedding similarity, token-level surprisal, and self-critique signals into continuous per-item exposure scores applicable to any benchmark without auxiliary data requirements.
15	    \item \textbf{Detection signal failure modes.} We document a directional reversal in Min-K\%++ at model scales below 1.5B parameters (Cohen's $d = -1.55$ to $-1.98$), where the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective. This finding has immediate practical consequences for contamination detection at small scales.
16	    \item \textbf{Identifiability boundaries and domain-level diagnosis.} Simulation studies establish that reliable $\gamma$ recovery requires signal AUC $\geq 0.55$ and $J \geq 50$, a threshold well above current LLM evaluation practice ($J = 8$--$30$). At our empirical $J = 13$, per-domain $\gamma$ estimates reveal directional heterogeneity across MMLU cognitive domains, with one domain (Social Sciences, $\gamma = -0.57$) reaching significance and providing preliminary evidence that contamination effects vary by cognitive demand. These results validate the identifiability analysis and chart a path toward domain-specific evaluation with larger model pools.
17	\end{enumerate}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01PPnf1A9fNNa9gu2dP8yPDe`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}\label{sec:conclusion}
2	
3	We presented ContamCal, a framework that connects contamination detection signals to IRT-based calibration through a two-stage pipeline: training-free signal fusion via RRF followed by contamination-augmented IRT estimation. Our experiments on MMLU, ARC-Challenge, and GSM8K with controlled contamination injection across the Qwen2.5 family yield three findings. First, IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of the contamination parameter $\gamma$, reducing calibration error by up to 53\% relative to raw scores. This decomposition shows that the primary value of psychometric modeling for contaminated benchmarks lies in separating stable ability from item-level noise. Second, Min-K\%++ exhibits directional reversal at small model scales: the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective. Third, simulation studies and empirical results at $J = 13$ confirm that reliable per-domain $\gamma$ recovery requires ensemble sizes ($J \geq 50$) well above current LLM evaluation practice; at our $J = 13$, per-domain analysis reveals directional heterogeneity across MMLU cognitive domains, with one domain (Social Sciences) reaching significance and charting a path toward domain-specific evaluation with larger model pools.
4	
5	The central lesson is that IRT provides a principled calibration framework for contaminated benchmarks, with per-domain $\gamma$ parameters offering diagnostic potential that will become increasingly actionable as evaluation pools grow. Scaling to $J \geq 50$ through broader model sampling, multidimensional IRT for domains where scalar $\gamma$ is insufficient, and active signal selection for closed-source settings are immediate next steps.
```

</details>

      "content": "\\section{Conclusion}\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe presented ContamCal, a framework that connects contamination detection signals to IRT-based calibration through a two-stage pipeline: training-free signal fusion via RRF followed by contamination-augmented IRT estimation. Our experiments on MMLU, ARC-Challenge, and GSM8K with controlled contamination injection across the Qwen2.5 family yield three findings. First, IRT ability estimation provides calibration benefit independent of the contamination parameter $\\gamma$, reducing calibration error by up to 53\\% relative to raw scores. This decomposition shows that the primary value of psychometric modeling for contaminated benchmarks lies in separating stable ability from item-level noise. Second, Min-K\\%++ exhibits directional reversal at small model scales: the signal is actively misleading rather than merely ineffective. Third, simulation studies and empirical results at $J = 13$ confirm that reliable per-domain $\\gamma$ recovery requires ensemble sizes ($J \\geq 50$) well above current LLM evaluation practice; at our $J = 13$, per-domain analysis reveals directional heterogeneity across MMLU cognitive domains, with one domain (Social Sciences) reaching significance and charting a path toward domain-specific evaluation with larger model pools.\n\nThe central lesson is that IRT provides a principled calibration framework for contaminated benchmarks, with per-domain $\\gamma$ parameters offering diagnostic potential that will become increasingly actionable as evaluation pools grow. Scaling to $J \\geq 50$ through broader model sampling, multidimensional IRT for domains where scalar $\\gamma$ is insufficient, and active signal selection for closed-source settings are immediate next steps.",
      "numLines": 5,
      "startLine": 1,
      "totalLines": 5
    }
  },

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	We discuss limitations in Appendix~\ref{sec:limitations}, including scale ceiling (all models $\leq$3B), signal coverage gaps for closed-source models, ensemble size constraints ($J=13$ vs.\ the $J \geq 50$ needed for reliable $\gamma$ recovery), position bias confounds at the 0.5B scale, and under-coverage of posterior credible intervals.
5	
6	\paragraph{Implications for practice.}
7	Our findings carry four recommendations. For benchmark developers, IRT-based calibration should be standard infrastructure: vanilla 2PL alone reduces MAE by 53\% on ARC without any detection signals. For detection practitioners, Min-K\%++ should not be trusted below 1.5B parameters due to directional reversal (\S\ref{sec:signal_failure}). For leaderboard operators, adding IRT to evaluation pipelines is a low-cost improvement that does not require contamination detection. For researchers, per-domain breakdowns should be reported alongside aggregate scores, as contamination may affect cognitive domains differently.
8	
9	\paragraph{Future directions.}
10	Scaling to $J \geq 50$ by pooling respondents across checkpoints, seeds, and training configurations would enable confirmatory tests of the directional heterogeneity observed here. Multidimensional IRT could model domain-specific ability dimensions that interact differently with contamination. Active signal selection via Fisher information optimization could reduce the requirement for full corpus access, enabling application to closed-source models.
11

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/conclusion.tex:5:The central lesson is that IRT provides a principled calibration framework for contaminated benchmarks, with per-domain $\gamma$ parameters offering diagnostic potential that will become increasingly actionable as evaluation pools grow. Scaling to $J \geq 50$ through broader model sampling, multidimensional IRT for domains where scalar $\gamma$ is insufficient, and active signal selection for closed-source settings are immediate next steps.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/method.tex:11:Our goal is twofold: (1)~estimate $\theta_j^{\text{clean}}$, the ability each model would exhibit had it never encountered test items during training, and (2)~diagnose contamination effects at domain granularity within each benchmark. We draw on item compromise IRT \citep{eckerly_2017_item_compromise,sinharay_2017_preknowledge} and explanatory IRT \citep{deboeck_2004_explanatory_irt}, adapting these frameworks through three structural modifications for LLM evaluation.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/method.tex:17:\textit{Adaptation~3: Stable ability $\to$ regime-conditional ability.} In educational testing, ability is approximately constant. For LLMs, the training regime (SFT vs.\ RL) qualitatively changes both internal representations and signal reliability. We recover clean ability via counterfactual inference (setting exposure to zero) rather than regime-specific ability parameters.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/method.tex:22:[Omitted long matching line]

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex:11:We exclude 0.5B models from MMLU and ARC IRT analyses due to answer-position bias that correlates with dosage (A-option rate: 46\% at d000 to 98\% at d100). Three 3B checkpoints are excluded for pipeline inconsistencies (details in Appendix~\ref{sec:exclusion_details}), yielding $J=13$ respondents (1.5B$\times$8 + 3B$\times$5). The eight 1.5B d000 models serve as the clean reference anchor; 3B ability estimates are anchored via joint fitting with these controls. GSM8K retains all scales.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/experimental_setup.tex:16:To test whether IRT calibration is robust to inclusion of new model families, we fine-tune Llama-3.2-3B \citep{grattafiori2024llama3} under the same protocol (d000, d005, d025, d100) with a single seed, yielding $J=4$ additional models. This analysis is \textbf{exploratory}: the single-seed design provides limited statistical power, and we report post-hoc power calculations in Appendix~E using Qwen $J=13$ effect sizes as reference.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/results.tex:8:[Omitted long matching line]

/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/method.tex:62:The reliability of $\gamma$ estimation depends on two factors: signal discriminative quality (AUC of exposure scores) and ensemble size~$J$. When signal AUC is low, exposure scores become uniform, collapsing this variance and rendering $\gamma$ structurally unidentifiable.
/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_13/projects/contamcal-irt-calibration/docs/paper/discussion.tex:4:We discuss limitations in Appendix~\ref{sec:limitations}, including scale ceiling (all models $\leq$3B), signal coverage gaps for closed-source models, ensemble size constraints ($J=13$ vs.\ the $J \geq 50$ needed for reliable $\gamma$ recovery), position bias confounds at the 0.5B scale, and under-coverage of posterior credible intervals.

15	\textit{Adaptation~2: Binary leakage $\to$ continuous exposure.} Item compromise IRT uses binary indicators \citep{sinharay_2017_preknowledge}.
